7.
Proofs of main results

7.1.
The ring of covariants

Our first task is to prove Theorems
3.7
,
3.13
, and
3.17
.
The starting point of our proofs is the standard monomial theory developed in
[
27
]
.
Since the language and notation in
[
27
]
differ from our approach in the present paper, we first take care to align the two approaches before giving the main proofs.

In the following discussion, let
H=\GL_{k}
or
\Sp_{2k}
.
(The group
\operatorname{O}_{k}
requires special treatment in Section
7.4
.)
Let
N
be the maximal unipotent subgroup of
H
defined in Sections
2.2.1
–
2.2.2
.
In
[
27
]
, the
\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}
is called the
ring of covariants
.
This ring effectively gathers all the modules of covariants into a single object, as follows.
Taking the
N
-invariants in the Howe duality decomposition (
2.20
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{\tau\in\Sigma}(\mathbb{C}[W]\otimes U_{\tau})^{H}\otimes(U_{\tau}^{*})^{N},

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{\tau\in\Sigma}(\mathbb{C}[W]\otimes U_{\tau})^{H}\otimes\mathbb{C}u^{*}_{\tau},

where
u^{*}_{\tau}
is a highest weight vector in
U^{*}_{\tau}
.
Hence as a
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\tau\in\Sigma}\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}_{\tau^{*}}

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{\tau\in\Sigma}(\mathbb{C}[W]\otimes U_{\tau})^{H},

where
\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}_{\tau^{*}}
is defined to contain precisely those functions in
\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}
which are weight vectors under the action of the maximal torus of
H
, having weight equal to the highest weight of
U_{\tau}^{*}
.
(In terms of highest weights, the distinction between
U_{\tau}
and
U_{\tau}^{*}
is relevant only when
H=\GL_{k}
.)
Explicitly, the isomorphism above is given by

(7.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi:(\mathbb{C}[W]\otimes U_{\tau})^{H}&\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}_{\tau^{*}},\\
\varphi&\longmapsto u^{*}_{\tau}\circ\varphi,\end{split}

where
\varphi
is viewed as a function
\varphi:W\longrightarrow U_{\tau}
.

We start with the following sketch of our upcoming proofs.
The main result of
[
27
]
is a
standard monomial theory
for the ring of covariants
\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}
.
In brief, this means that
\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}
is a quotient of the polynomial algebra generated by the
f_{ij}
’s and by certain functions
\det_{I}\>X
and
\det_{J}\>Y
(to be defined below), where the defining ideal
\mathcal{I}
is generated by certain monomials.
The monomials lying outside
\mathcal{I}
are called
standard monomials
, and they furnish a linear basis for
\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}
(after we identify them with their images in the quotient).
The results in
[
27
]
give the minimal set of generators for
\mathcal{I}
, which we will reinterpret in terms of our jellyfish diagrams (where products of
\det
functions are still depicted by tentacles).
We then will show that the standard monomials — which are necessarily products of
f_{ij}
’s and
\det
functions — are precisely those monomials that lie inside some jellyfish.
By restricting our attention to the component
\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}_{\tau^{*}}
, we need only consider the jellyfish of shape
\tau
.
By viewing the families
\mathbf{F}
as the facets of a shellable order complex, we obtain a Stanley decomposition of the Stanley–Reisner ring, which leads to the desired Stanley decomposition of the module of covariants.
We give the proof for
H=\Sp_{2k}
first, since it is simpler but identical in spirit to the proof for
\GL_{k}
.

7.2.
The symplectic group

Let
X=[x_{ij}]
be the
k\times n
matrix of coordinate functions on
W=V^{n}
described in Section
A.2
.
Let
I
be a tableau column of length
m\leq k
, with entries in
[n]
.
We define
\det_{I}\!X\in\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}
to be the determinant of the
m\times m
minor of
X
whose column indices are the entries in
I
, and whose row indices are
k+1,k+2,\ldots,k+m
.
Therefore, as a weight vector for both
H
and
\mathfrak{g}
(see Section
A.2
), we have

\displaystyle{\rm wt}_{H}\left(\textstyle\det_{I}\!X\right)

\displaystyle=\omega_{m},

\displaystyle{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\textstyle\det_{I}\!X\right)

\displaystyle=-(k^{n})-\sum_{i\in I}\epsilon_{i}.

Given a tableau
T\in\SSYT(\tau,n)
, let
\det_{T}\!X
denote the product of the functions
\det_{I}\!X
, taken over all columns
I
in
T
.
It follows from the weights above that

(7.2)

\displaystyle{\rm wt}_{H}\left(\textstyle\det_{T}\!X\right)

\displaystyle=\tau,

(7.3)

\displaystyle{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\textstyle\det_{T}\!X\right)

\displaystyle=-(k^{n})-{\rm cont}(T),

where
{\rm cont}(T)
denotes the content of
T
as defined above (
2.12
).

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\varphi_{T}
be the map defined in (
2.14
).
The image of this map under the isomorphism
\Psi
in (
7.1
) is given by

\Psi:\varphi_{T}\longmapsto\textstyle\det_{T}\!X.

Proof.

Since the irreducible
\Sp_{2k}
-representation
U_{\tau}
is a quotient of the irreducible
\GL_{2k}
-representation with highest weight
\tau
, the proof follows from that of Lemma
7.6
below.
∎

Remark 7.2
.

In
[
27
]
*Def. 3.3.5, the functions which we call
\det_{I}\!X
are denoted by
\beta_{I}
.
Likewise, in the proof for
H=\GL_{k}
we will introduce functions
\det_{J}\!Y
which in
[
27
]
*Def. 3.3.5 are denoted by
\alpha_{J}
.
Our products
\det_{T}\!X
and
\det_{T}\!Y
are denoted there by
m_{\beta}(T)
and
m_{\alpha}(T)
, respectively.

Definition 7.3

(see
[
27
]
*Def. 3.6.16)
.

An
\Sp_{2k}
-split
is an arrangement of positive integers from the set
[n]
, taking the form

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&a_{1}&\\
&\vee&\\
&\vdots&\\
&\vee&\\
&a_{s}&\\
&\vee&\\
b_{r}>\cdots>b_{1}>c_{1}>\cdots>c_{r}>&d_{1}&>\cdots>d_{t}\end{array}

such that
r\geq s
, and
s+t\leq 2k
, and
r+t=k+1
.
(The parameters
r,s,t
are allowed to be zero, meaning that the corresponding sequences are empty.)
The
monomial
of a split is

(7.11)

\prod_{i=1}^{r}f_{c_{i},b_{i}}\cdot\textstyle\det_{\{d_{t},\ldots,d_{1},a_{s},\ldots,a_{1}\}}X.

Lemma 7.4

(see
[
27
]
*Thms. 3.6.17 and 3.8.12)
.

We have

\mathbb{C}[V^{n}]^{N}=\mathbb{C}\big[\{f_{ij}\}\cup\{\textstyle\det_{I}\!X\}\big]\big/\mathcal{I},

where
\mathcal{I}
is the ideal generated by

•

all monomials of
\Sp_{2k}
-splits;

•

all products
\det_{I}\!X\det_{I^{\prime}}X
where
I
and
I^{\prime}
are incomparable in the tableau order.

Moreover, the images of the monomials lying outside
\mathcal{I}
(i.e., the standard monomials) furnish a linear basis for
\mathbb{C}[V^{n}]^{N}
.

It follows from (
7.2
) and Lemma
7.4
that every standard monomial lying in
\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}_{\tau^{*}}
must be a product of
f_{ij}
’s with
\det_{T}\!X
, for some tableau
T\in\SSYT(\tau,n)
; the semistandard condition is forced because a standard monomial cannot be divisible by a product of incomparable
\det
functions (otherwise it would lie inside
\mathcal{I}
).
We will say that the
support
of a monomial
\mathbf{m}
is the set of points
(i,j)\in\mathcal{P}
such that
f_{ij}
divides
\mathbf{m}
.
Moreover, to clarify the proof below, we make
\mathcal{P}
a poset equipped with the product order, whereby
(i,j)\leq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
if and only if both
i\leq i^{\prime}
and
j\leq j^{\prime}
.
Note that each lattice path in a family
\mathbf{F}
is a chain.
Recall that an
antichain
in
\mathcal{P}
is a subset in which each pair of elements is incomparable, meaning that one lies strictly northeast of the other.
Hence, given an antichain contained in
\mathbf{F}
, each element in the antichain lies in a different lattice path of
\mathbf{F}
.
Note that the support of the monomial (
7.11
) of a split is an antichain of size
r
.
Given a subset of
\mathcal{P}
, its
width
is defined as the size of the largest antichain it contains.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
T\in\SSYT(\tau,n)
, and let
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}[f_{ij}]\det_{T}\!X
be a monomial.
Then
\mathbf{m}
is a standard monomial if and only if its support is contained in some jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T
.

Proof.

As in Definition
3.11
, let
E
be the unique endpoint set such that
E\rightrightarrows T
.
Thus in any jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T
, we have
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows E
.
Since
\mathbf{F}
consists of
k
nonintersecting lattice paths with endpoints given by
E
, it must be the case that for all
1\leq t\leq k
, the first
t
lattice paths all lie weakly north of the
t
th endpoint in
E
.
Equivalently, the subset of
\mathbf{F}
lying strictly south of this endpoint intersects precisely the last
k-t
lattice paths.
Therefore, the support of
\mathbf{m}
is contained in some jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T
if and only if the following condition holds for all
0\leq t\leq k
: the support of
\mathbf{m}
, when restricted to the part lying strictly south of the
t
th endpoint in
E
, has width at most
k-t
.
(We define the 0th endpoint in
E
to be 0.)
But we can restate this condition in terms of the first column of
T
rather than in terms of
E
, due to the following observation from (
3.9
):
the part of
\mathcal{P}
lying strictly south of the
t
th endpoint in
E_{\max}
has width
k-t
.
Combining this with the construction of
E
in Definition
3.11
,
we obtain the following (where
T_{0,1}\coloneqq 0
):

(7.12)

The support of
\mathbf{m}
is contained in some jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T

\Longleftrightarrow

for all
0\leq t\leq\ell(\tau)
,

the support of
\mathbf{m}
lying strictly south of
T_{t,1}
has width at most
k-t
.

On one hand, then, suppose that the support of
\mathbf{m}
is not contained in any jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T
.
That is, by (
7.12
), there is some
t
such that the support of
\mathbf{m}
lying strictly south of
T_{t,1}
has width at least
r\coloneqq k-t+1
(note that this coincides with the parameter
r
in a split, as in Definition
7.3
).
In other words, the support of
\mathbf{m}
contains some antichain
\{(c_{1},b_{1}),\ldots,(c_{r},b_{r})\}
, written in order from southwest to northeast, such that
c_{r}>T_{t,1}
.
Therefore, if we set
\{d_{t},\ldots,d_{1},a_{s},\ldots,a_{1}\}
equal to the first column of
T
(so that
d_{1}=T_{t,1}
), then
\mathbf{m}
is divisible by the monomial (
7.11
) of a split.
Hence we have
\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{I}
, and so
\mathbf{m}
is not a standard monomial.

On the other hand, suppose that the support of
\mathbf{m}
is contained in some jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T
.
Then there is no such
t
violating the width condition in (
7.12
), and so it is impossible to construct a split (as we did above) whose monomial divides
\mathbf{m}
.
Thus
\mathbf{m}\notin\mathcal{I}
and so
\mathbf{m}
is a standard monomial. ∎

In the proof below, we appeal to the theory of edge labelings and induced shellings described in
[
4
]
*Thm. 2.3 and
[
3
]
*Def. 2.1.
See also
[
46
]
*Ch. 3 for a general reference on simplicial complexes and Stanley–Reisner rings.

Proof of Theorem
3.13
.

Let
T\in\SSYT(\tau,n)
.
By Lemma
7.5
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[V^{n}]^{N}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{T\in\SSYT(\tau,n)}{\rm span}\Big\{\text{standard monomials in $\mathbb{C}[f_{ij}]\textstyle\det_{T}\!X$}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{T\in\SSYT(\tau,n)}{\rm span}\bigcup_{\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T}\Big\{\text{monomials in }\mathbb{C}[f_{ij}:(i,j)\in\mathbf{F}]\textstyle\det_{T}\!X\Big\}.

Applying the inverse
\Psi^{-1}
of the isomorphism in (
7.1
) via Lemma
7.1
, we have

(7.13)

(\mathbb{C}[V^{n}]\otimes U_{\tau})^{\Sp_{2k}}=\bigoplus_{T\in\SSYT(\tau,n)}{\rm span}\bigcup_{\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T}\Big\{\text{monomials in }\mathbb{C}[f_{ij}:(i,j)\in\mathbf{F}]\textstyle\Big\}\cdot\varphi_{T}.

Let
E
be the unique endpoint set such that
E\rightrightarrows T
.
We consider the abstract simplicial complex
\Delta_{E}
in which each face is a subset of some family
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows E
.
The monomials appearing in each component of (
7.13
) are precisely those monomials whose support is a face of
\Delta_{E}
.
Therefore, letting
\mathbb{C}[\Delta_{E}]
denote the Stanley–Reisner ring of
\Delta_{E}
, we can rewrite (
7.13
) as

(7.14)

(\mathbb{C}[V^{n}]\otimes U_{\tau})^{\Sp_{2k}}=\bigoplus_{E\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{C}[\Delta_{E}]\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}T:\\
E\rightrightarrows T\end{subarray}}\varphi_{T}.

The families
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows E
are the facets of
\Delta_{E}
, which is pure since each such
\mathbf{F}
has the same size
d_{E}
.
To assign a shelling order to the facets, we define the following binary edge labeling on an individual lattice path.
We label a step with “0” if we step toward the east, or if we step into a shadow; otherwise we label a step with “1.”
In this way, each lattice path has a
label sequence
of 0’s and 1’s.
A
descent
in a label sequence is an occurrence of a 1 followed by a 0.
By concatenating the label sequences of the
k
lattice paths in
\mathbf{F}
(from southwest to northeast), we associate a binary sequence to
\mathbf{F}
.
A shelling order on the facets is obtained by ordering these binary sequences lexicographically.
In this shelling order, the restriction of each facet
\mathbf{F}
is given by the union of the descent sets of the label sequences of the
k
lattice paths in
\mathbf{F}
.
It follows from (
3.3
) that the restriction of
\mathbf{F}
is precisely the set
{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F})
.
Note that the first facet
\mathbf{F}_{0}
in the shelling order has no descents, and thus indeed
{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F}_{0})=\varnothing
.

As a concrete example before completing the proof, take
k=2
and
n=7
, and let
E=\{2,4\}
.
Then
\Delta_{E}
has 17 facets.
Below, we display the first few facets in the aforementioned shelling order, writing the label sequence beneath each facet
\mathbf{F}
and underlining each descent therein.
In the label sequence for each facet
\mathbf{F}
, for the sake of clarity, we insert a space between the label sequence of the first and second lattice path in
\mathbf{F}
:

00000001 0001

<

000001
10
0001

<

0000
10
01 0001

<

0000
10
01 00
10

<

0000
10
10
0001

<\cdots

The rest of the shelling order is shown below (where we suppress the label sequences to save space):

By
[
48
]
*Lemma 1, each shelling of an abstract simplicial complex induces a Stanley decomposition of its Stanley–Reisner ring.
In the present context, the shelling described above induces the following Stanley decomposition of
\mathbb{C}[\Delta_{E}]
:

\mathbb{C}[\Delta_{E}]=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows E}\mathbb{C}[f_{ij}:(i,j)\in\mathbf{F}]f_{{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F})}.

Substituting this in (
7.14
), and then combining the indices
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows E
and
E\rightrightarrows T
to obtain a single direct sum over all jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T
, we obtain the Stanley decomposition (
3.10
) in Theorem
3.13
.

It remains to prove the formula (
3.11
) for the Hilbert–Poincaré series.
This is done by re-expanding (
3.10
) as the triple sum

(\mathbb{C}[V^{n}]\otimes U_{\tau})^{\Sp(V)}=\bigoplus_{E\in\mathcal{E}}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}T:\\
E\rightrightarrows T\end{subarray}}\varphi_{T}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{F}:\\
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows E\end{subarray}}\mathbb{C}[f_{ij}:(i,j)\in\mathbf{F}]f_{{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F})}.

It is clear that
P_{E}(t)
, as defined in (
3.2
), is the Hilbert–Poincaré series of the innermost direct sum
\bigoplus_{\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows E}\mathbb{C}[f_{ij}:(i,j)\in\mathbf{F}]f_{{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F})}
.
(Recall that each
f_{ij}
has degree 2 as an element of
\mathbb{C}[V^{n}]
, which explains the
t^{2}
.)
There are as many copies of this component as there are summands in the second direct sum, namely the number of tableaux
T\in\SSYT(\tau,n)
such that
E\rightrightarrows T
, which by Definition
3.11
equals
\#\tau_{E}
.
As we observed after (
2.14
), the map
\varphi_{T}
has degree
|\tau|
, so we multiply through by
t^{|\tau|}
, and the result (
3.11
) follows.
∎

7.3.
The general linear group

All arguments below are identical to those for the symplectic group above, but there are several details that must be adapted.

Let
X=[x_{ij}]
and
Y=[y_{ij}]
be the
k\times q
and (respectively)
p\times k
matrices of coordinate functions on
W=V^{*p}\oplus V^{q}
described in Section
A.1
.
Let
I
be a tableau column of length
m\leq k
, with entries in
[q]
.
We define
\det_{I}\!X
to be the determinant of the
m\times m
minor of
X
whose column indices are the entries in
I
, and whose rows are the last
m
rows of
X
.
Similarly, let
J
be a tableau column of length
m\leq k
, with entries in
[p]
.
We define
\det_{J}\!Y
to be the determinant of the
m\times m
minor of
Y
whose row indices are the entries in
J
, and whose columns are the first
m
columns of
Y
.
It is straightforward to check that
\det_{I}\!X
and
\det_{J}\!Y
are elements of
\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}
.
Moreover, as weight vectors (see Section
A.1
), one has

\displaystyle{\rm wt}_{H}\left(\textstyle\det_{I}\!X\right)

\displaystyle=-\overleftarrow{\omega_{m}},

\displaystyle{\rm wt}_{H}\left(\textstyle\det_{J}\!Y\right)

\displaystyle=\omega_{m},

\displaystyle{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\textstyle\det_{I}\!X\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-(k^{p})\>\Bigg|\>\sum_{i\in I}\epsilon_{i}\right),

\displaystyle{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\textstyle\det_{J}\!Y\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-(k^{p})-\sum_{j\in J}\epsilon_{j}\>\Bigg|\>0\right).

Given
(T^{+},T^{-})\in\SSYT(\tau^{+},q)\times\SSYT(\tau^{-},p)
, let
\det_{T^{+}}\!X
denote the product of the functions
\det_{I}\!X
, taken over all columns
I
in
T^{+}
; likewise, let
\det_{T^{-}}\!Y
denote the product of the functions
\det_{J}\!Y
, taken over all columns
J
in
T^{-}
.
It follows from the weights above that

\displaystyle{\rm wt}_{H}\left(\textstyle\det_{T^{+}}\!X\right)

\displaystyle=-\overleftarrow{\tau^{+}},

\displaystyle{\rm wt}_{H}\left(\textstyle\det_{T^{-}}\!Y\right)

\displaystyle=\tau^{-},

\displaystyle{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\textstyle\det_{T^{+}}\!X\right)

\displaystyle=\Big(\!-(k^{p})\mid{\rm cont}(T^{+})\Big),

\displaystyle{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\textstyle\det_{T^{-}}\!Y\right)

\displaystyle=\Big(\!-(k^{p})-{\rm cont}(T^{-})\>\Big|\;0^{q}\Big).

We observe that
{\rm wt}_{H}(\det_{T^{+}}\!X\det_{T^{-}}\!Y)=\tau^{-}-\overleftarrow{\tau}^{+}=-\overleftarrow{\tau}
is indeed the highest weight of
U_{\tau}^{*}
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
\varphi_{(T^{+},T^{-})}
be the map defined in (
2.12
).
The image of this map under the isomorphism
\Psi
in (
7.1
) is given by

\Psi:\varphi_{(T^{+},T^{-})}\longmapsto\textstyle\det_{T^{+}}\!\cdot X\det_{T^{-}}\!Y.

Proof.

Let
\tau
be the shape of
(T^{+},T^{-})
.
Let
A^{+}\in\SSYT(\tau^{+},k)
be the tableau obtained by filling each column from bottom to top with the entries
k,k-1,k-2,\ldots
, and let
A^{-}\in\SSYT(\tau^{-},k)
be the tableau obtained by filling each column from top to bottom with the entries
1,2,3,\ldots
.
Then
(A^{+},A^{-})
is a rational tableau of shape
\tau
, and thus is a basis element for
U_{\tau}
via the identification (
2.11
).
The linear functional sending an element of
U_{\tau}
to the coefficient of
(A^{+},A^{-})
has weight
-\overleftarrow{\tau}
, which is the highest weight of
U_{\tau}^{*}
; therefore we can take
u^{*}_{\tau}
to be this functional.
A straightforward calculation verifies that
\det_{T^{+}}\!X\cdot\det_{T^{-}}\!Y
is indeed the coefficient of
(A^{+},A^{-})
in
\varphi_{(T^{+},T^{-})}(v^{*}_{1},\ldots,v^{*}_{p},v_{1},\ldots,v_{q})
.
∎

Definition 7.7

(see
[
27
]
*Def. 3.6.15)
.

A
\GL_{k}
-split
is an arrangement of positive integers taking the form

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&a^{*}_{1}&\\
&\vee&\\
&\vdots&\\
&\vee&\\
&a^{*}_{s}&\\
&\vee&\\
b_{r}>\cdots>b_{1}\geq c^{*}_{1}>\cdots>c^{*}_{r}>&d^{*}_{1}&>\cdots>d^{*}_{t}\end{array}\hskip 28.45274pt\begin{array}[]{rcl}&a_{1}&\\
&\vee&\\
&\vdots&\\
&\vee&\\
&a_{v}&\\
&\vee&\\
b^{*}_{u}>\cdots>b^{*}_{1}>c_{1}>\cdots>c_{u}>&d_{1}&>\cdots>d_{w},\end{array}

and subject to the following conditions:

(1)

the starred entries are elements of
[p]
, and the unstarred entries are elements of
[q]
;

(2)

r\geq s
and
u\geq v
;

(3)

s+t\leq k
and
v+w\leq k
;

(4)

r+t+u+w=k+1
;

(5)

r+u>0
;

(6)

b_{1}>c_{1}
and
b^{*}_{1}>c^{*}_{1}
.

(The parameters
r,s,t,u,v,w
are allowed to be zero, meaning that the corresponding sequences are empty.)
The
monomial
of a split is

(7.15)

\prod_{i=1}^{r}f_{c^{*}_{i},b_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{u}f_{b^{*}_{i},c_{i}}\cdot\textstyle\det_{\{d_{w},\ldots,d_{1},a_{v},\ldots,a_{1}\}}\!X\cdot\det_{\{d^{*}_{t},\ldots,d^{*}_{1},a^{*}_{s},\ldots,a^{*}_{1}\}}\!Y.

Lemma 7.8

(see
[
27
]
*Thms. 3.6.17 and 3.8.27)
.

We have

\mathbb{C}[V^{*p}\oplus V^{q}]^{N}=\mathbb{C}\big[\{f_{ij}\}\cup\{\textstyle\det_{I}\!X\}\cup\{\textstyle\det_{J}\!Y\}\big]\big/\mathcal{I},

where
\mathcal{I}
is the ideal generated by

•

all monomials of
\GL_{k}
-splits;

•

all products
\det_{I}\!X\cdot\det_{I^{\prime}}\!X
where
I
and
I^{\prime}
are incomparable in the tableau order;

•

all products
\det_{J}\!Y\cdot\det_{J^{\prime}}\!X
where
J
and
J^{\prime}
are incomparable in the tableau order;

•

all products
\det_{I}\!X\cdot\det_{J}\!Y
where
\#I+\#J>k
.

Moreover, the images of the monomials lying outside
\mathcal{I}
(i.e., the standard monomials) furnish a linear basis for
\mathbb{C}[V^{*p}\oplus V^{q}]^{N}
.

It follows from Lemma
7.8
that every standard monomial lying in
\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}_{\tau}
must be a product of
f_{ij}
’s with some
\det_{T^{+}}\!X\cdot\det_{T^{-}}\!Y
, for some pair
(T^{+},T^{-})\in\SSYT(\tau^{+},q)\times\SSYT(\tau^{-},p)
.
Just as for
\Sp_{2k}
in the previous section, we again make
\mathcal{P}
a poset where
(i,j)\leq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
if and only if both
i\leq i^{\prime}
and
j\leq j^{\prime}
; in this way, each lattice path in a family
\mathbf{F}
forms a chain.
Note that the support of the monomial (
7.15
) of a split is an antichain of size
r+u
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
(T^{+},T^{-})\in\SSYT(\tau^{+},q)\times\SSYT(\tau^{-},p)
, and let

\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}[f_{ij}]\textstyle\det_{T^{+}}\!X\det_{T^{-}}\!Y

be a monomial.
Then
\mathbf{m}
is a standard monomial if and only if its support is contained in some jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows(T^{+},T^{-})
.

Proof.

As in Definition
3.4
, let
E
be the unique endpoint set such that
E\rightrightarrows(T^{+},T^{-})
.
Thus in any jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows(T^{+},T^{-})
, we have
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows E
.
Therefore, the support of
\mathbf{m}
is contained in some jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows(T^{+},T^{-})
if and only if the following condition holds for all
0\leq t\leq\ell(\tau^{-})
and
0\leq w\leq k-\ell(\tau^{-})
:
the support of
\mathbf{m}
, when restricted to the rectangle lying strictly south of the
t
th endpoint in
E\cap[p]^{*}
and strictly east of the
w
th endpoint in
E\cap[q]
, has width at most
k-t-w
.
(Define the “0th” endpoint to be 0.)
We can restate this condition in terms of the first columns of
T^{+}
and
T^{-}
, due to the following observation (with
E^{\prime}
as in Definition
3.4
):
the rectangle lying strictly south of the
t
th endpoint in
E\cap[p]^{*}
and strictly east of the
w
th endpoint in
E^{\prime}\cap[q]
has width at most
k-t-w
.
We thus have the following (where
T^{-}_{0,1}
and
T^{+}_{0,1}
are both defined to be
0
):

(7.16)

The support of
\mathbf{m}
is contained in some jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows(T^{+},T^{-})

\Longleftrightarrow

for all
0\leq t\leq\ell(\tau^{-})
and
0\leq t\leq k-\ell(\tau^{-})
,

the support of
\mathbf{m}
lying strictly south of
(T^{-}_{t,1})^{*}
and strictly east of
T^{+}_{w,1}

has width at most
k-t-w
.

On one hand, then, suppose that the support of
\mathbf{m}
is not contained in any jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows(T^{+},T^{-})
.
That is, by (
7.16
), there is some
t
and
w
such that the support of
\mathbf{m}
lying strictly south of
T^{-}_{t,1}
and strictly east of
T^{+}_{w,1}
has width at least
k-t-w+1
.
In other words, for some
r,u
such that
r+u=k-t-w+1
, the support of
\mathbf{m}
contains an antichain
\{(b_{u}^{*},c_{u}),\ldots,(b_{1}^{*},c_{1})\}\cup\{(c^{*}_{1},b_{1}),\ldots,(c^{*}_{r},b_{r})\}
, written in order from southwest to northeast, such that both
c^{*}_{r}>T^{-}_{t,1}
and
c_{u}>T^{+}_{w,1}
; here we have written the antichain as the union of its two parts lying strictly below (resp., weakly above) the main diagonal in
\mathcal{P}
.
Therefore, if we set
\{d_{t}^{*},\ldots,d_{1}^{*},a_{s}^{*},\ldots,a_{1}^{*}\}
equal to the first column of
T^{-}
(so that
d_{1}^{*}=T^{-}_{t,1}
), and set
\{d_{w},\ldots,d_{1},a_{v},\ldots,a_{1}\}
equal to the first column of
T^{+}
(so that
d_{1}=T^{+}_{w,1}
), then
\mathbf{m}
is divisible by the monomial (
7.15
) of a split.
(Note that all six parts of Definition
7.7
are satisfied.)
Hence we have
\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{I}
, and so
\mathbf{m}
is not a standard monomial.

On the other hand, suppose that the support of
\mathbf{m}
is contained in some jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows(T^{+},T^{-})
.
Then there is no such
t,w
violating the width condition in (
7.16
), and so it is impossible to construct a split (as we did above) whose monomial divides
\mathbf{m}
.
Thus
\mathbf{m}\notin\mathcal{I}
and so
\mathbf{m}
is a standard monomial.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.7
.

In the same way as we obtained (
7.14
), we can use Lemma
7.9
to obtain

(\mathbb{C}[V^{*p}\oplus V^{q}]\otimes U_{\tau})^{\GL(V)}=\bigoplus_{E\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{C}[\Delta_{E}]\bigoplus_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}(T^{+},T^{-}):\\
E\rightrightarrows(T^{+},T^{-})\end{subarray}}}\varphi_{(T^{+},T^{-})}.

The shelling of
\Delta_{E}
is defined identically as in the proof of Theorem
3.13
, and the rest of the proof proceeds analogously.
∎

7.4.
The orthogonal group

Ultimately, the proof for the orthogonal group is similar to the other two groups above, but due to technicalities involving highest weight vectors, we will not begin by working inside the ring
\mathbb{C}[W]^{N}
.
For the same reason, the standard monomial theory developed for
\operatorname{O}_{k}
in
[
27
]
is restricted to the case
k>2n
(see pp. 64 and 75), and so the analogue of the splits above were not defined.
See also the discussion in our introduction (Section
1.5
) regarding several complications with the representations of
\operatorname{O}_{k}
.
We begin by introducing an analogue of the splits for the orthogonal group:

Definition 7.10
.

An
\operatorname{O}_{k}
-split
is an arrangement of positive integers from the set
[n]
, taking the form

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&a_{1}&\\
&\wedge&\\
&\vdots&\\
&\wedge&\\
&a_{s}&\\
&\wedge&\\
b_{1}<\cdots<b_{r}<&d_{1}&<\cdots<d_{t}\\
\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\geq$}\hskip 12.0pt\hskip 27.98602pt\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\geq$}&&\\
c_{1}<\cdots<c_{r}&&\end{array}\vskip 4.30554pt

such that
r\geq s
, and
s+t=m
, and
r+t=k+1
.
Note that each
b_{i}\leq c_{i}
.
(The parameters
r,s,t
are allowed to be zero, meaning that the corresponding sequences are empty.)
The
monomial
of a split is

(7.26)

\prod_{i=1}^{r}f_{b_{i},c_{i}}\cdot\textstyle\varphi_{\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{s},d_{1},\ldots,d_{t}\}}.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
\dim V=k
.
Let
A=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{s}\}
,
B=\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}\}
,
C=\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{r}\}
, and
D=\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{t}\}
define an
\operatorname{O}_{k}
-split as in Definition
7.10
.
Let
f_{ij}
and
\varphi_{T}
be as defined in (
2.6
) and (
2.15
), respectively.
Let
\det_{B,C}[f_{ij}]
denote the determinant of the
r\times r
minor whose rows and columns are given by
B
and
C
, respectively.
Then

(7.27)

\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{|\sigma|}\>\sigma\Big(\textstyle\det_{B,C}[f_{ij}]\varphi_{A\cup D}\Big)=0,

where the sum ranges over all
\sigma
which act by exchanging a (possibly empty) subset of
D
with a subset of
B
of equal size.
The symbol
|\sigma|
denotes the common size of the exchanged subsets.

Proof.

We first claim that an analogous relation holds in the context of multilinear algebra, inside the space
\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\bigwedge\!$}}^{m}V\otimes\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\bigwedge\!$}}^{r}V
:

(7.28)

\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{|\sigma|}\>\sigma\cdot(u_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge u_{s}\wedge\underbrace{v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{t}}_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\sigma$ exchanges}\\
\text{some subset}\\
\text{of these vectors \ldots}\end{subarray}}})\otimes(\underbrace{w_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{r}}_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\ldots with the}\\
\text{same number}\\
\text{of these vectors}\end{subarray}}})=0,

where
|\sigma|
denotes the number of
v_{i}
’s (equivalently, the number of
w_{i}
’s) exchanged by
\sigma
.
To verify this, observe that the left-hand side of (
7.28
) is alternating in the vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{t}
, and also (independently) alternating in the vectors
w_{1},\ldots,w_{r}
.
The claim will follow if we show that the left-hand side of (
7.28
) is, in fact, alternating in all
r+t
of these vectors, since
r+t=k+1>k=\dim V
.
For this, it suffices to show that the left-hand side of (
7.28
) is alternating in the two vectors
v_{1}
and
w_{1}
.
To this end, consider the effect of exchanging
v_{1}
and
w_{1}
.
This changes the sign of all terms in which
v_{1}
and
w_{1}
appear in the same tensor factor.
Now consider a term in which
v_{1}
and
w_{1}
appear in opposite tensor factors.
This term is the result of the action of a certain
\sigma
;
exchanging
v_{1}
and
w_{1}
increments by
\pm 1
the number of
v_{i}
’s exchanged by
\sigma
, and therefore the sign of this term changes as well.
Thus, exchanging
v_{1}
and
w_{1}
changes the sign of every term on the left-hand side of (
7.28
), and so this expression is alternating in
v_{1}
and
w_{1}
, as desired.
This proves the claim (
7.28
).

Now we will translate (
7.28
) into a relation involving polynomial functions
V^{n}\longrightarrow\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\bigwedge\!$}}^{m}V
.
We will abbreviate
w\coloneqq(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\in V^{n}
.
The standard dot product on
V=\mathbb{C}^{k}
extends canonically to a symmetric bilinear form
\langle\;,\;\rangle
on
\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\bigwedge\!$}}^{r}V
, via

\big\langle v_{b_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{b_{r}},\>w_{c_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{c_{r}}\big\rangle\coloneqq\det[v_{b_{i}}\cdot w_{c_{j}}]_{i,j=1}^{r}.

Since by definition we have
f_{ij}:w\mapsto v_{i}\cdot v_{j}
, we can rewrite the function
\det_{B,C}[f_{ij}]:V^{n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
defined in the statement of this lemma, in terms of this form:

\textstyle\det_{B,C}[f_{ij}]:w\longmapsto\langle\varphi_{B}(w),\varphi_{C}(w)\rangle.

Therefore, we can view the function
\det_{B,C}[f_{ij}]\varphi_{A\cup D}:V^{n}\longrightarrow\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\bigwedge\!$}}^{m}V
as the result of factoring through the following contraction:

\displaystyle\textstyle\det_{B,C}[f_{ij}]\varphi_{A\cup D}:V^{n}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\bigwedge\!$}}^{m}V\otimes\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\bigwedge\!$}}^{r}V\otimes\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\bigwedge\!$}}^{r}V\longrightarrow\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\bigwedge\!$}}^{m}V,

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\longmapsto\varphi_{A\cup D}(w)\otimes\varphi_{B}(w)\otimes\varphi_{C}(w)\longmapsto\langle\varphi_{B}(w),\varphi_{C}(w)\rangle\>\varphi_{A\cup D}(w).

From this, we see that the relation (
7.28
) inside
\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\bigwedge\!$}}^{m}V\otimes\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\bigwedge\!$}}^{r}V
yields the relation (
7.27
).
∎

Remark 7.12
.

The relation (
7.28
) is known as a
Garnir relation
, which appears throughout the literature in various guises.
For
H=\Sp_{2k}
and
\GL_{k}
, the standard monomial results we used from
[
27
]
similarly depended on relations of this Garnir type; see equations (3.8.8) and (3.8.21) therein.

Although we are not working inside a ring, nonetheless we can impose a monomial ordering on the set of all monomials
\mathbf{m}\in\bigoplus_{T\in\SSYT((1^{m}),n)}\mathbb{C}[f_{ij}]\varphi_{T}
.
Given two such monomials
\mathbf{m}
and
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
, we first compare their factors
\varphi_{T}
and
\varphi_{T^{\prime}}
, so that
\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
if
T<T^{\prime}
with respect to lexicographical order.
If
T=T^{\prime}
, then we compare the
f_{ij}
’s in the lexicographical order, with respect to the ordering
f_{11}>f_{12}>\cdots>f_{1n}>f_{22}>f_{23}>\cdots>f_{nn}
.
The key fact is that the leading monomial on the left-hand side of (
7.27
) is the monomial of the split.
Hence the monomial of any split can be written as a linear combination of lesser monomials.
Adapting the language from the previous two subsections, we say that a monomial in
\bigoplus_{T\in\SSYT((1^{m}),n)}\mathbb{C}[f_{ij}]\varphi_{T}
is a
standard monomial
if it is not divisible by the monomial of a split.
It follows that the standard monomials furnish a linear basis for
(\mathbb{C}[V^{n}]\otimes\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\bigwedge\!$}}^{m}V)^{\operatorname{O}_{k}}
.

In the proof below, we now view
\mathcal{P}
as a poset with a different partial order than we used for the other two groups: in particular, we declare
(i,j)\leq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
if and only if
i\leq i^{\prime}
and
j\geq j^{\prime}
.
With respect to this partial order, each lattice path in a family
\mathbf{F}
(as defined in Definition
3.16
) forms a chain in
\mathcal{P}
.
Two elements of
\mathcal{P}
are incomparable if one lies strictly southeast of the other.
In particular, the support of the monomial (
7.26
) of a split is an antichain of size
r
.

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
T\in\SSYT((1^{m}),n)
, and let
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}[f_{ij}]\varphi_{T}
be a monomial.
Then
\mathbf{m}
is a standard monomial if and only if its support is contained in some jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T
.

Proof.

As in Definition
3.16
, let
E
be the unique endpoint set such that
E\rightrightarrows T
.
In any jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T
, it must be the case that for all
1\leq t\leq k
, the
t
southernmost lattice paths all lie weakly south of the
t
th greatest endpoint in
E
.
Equivalently, the subset of
\mathbf{F}
lying strictly north of this endpoint intersects precisely the northernmost
k-t
lattice paths.
Therefore, the support of
\mathbf{m}
is contained in some jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T
if and only if the following condition holds for all
0\leq t\leq k
: the support of
\mathbf{m}
, when restricted to the part lying strictly north of the
t
th greatest endpoint in
E
, has width at most
k-t
.
(By convention, all of
\mathcal{P}
lies north of the “0th” greatest endpoint of
E
.)
We can restate this condition in terms of
T
rather than in terms of
E
, since
T
is contained in
E
, and since the endpoints in
E\setminus T
lie as far north as possible (where again we regard all of
\mathcal{P}
as lying north of the “0th” greatest entry of
T
):

(7.29)

The support of
\mathbf{m}
is contained in some jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T

\Longleftrightarrow

for all
0\leq t\leq m
,

the support of
\mathbf{m}
lying strictly north of the
t
th greatest entry of
T

has width at most
k-t
.

On one hand, then, suppose that the support of
\mathbf{m}
is not contained in any jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T
.
That is, by (
7.29
), there is some
t
such that the support of
\mathbf{m}
lying strictly north of the
t
th greatest entry of
T
has width at least
r\coloneqq k-t+1
(note that this coincides with the parameter
r
in a split, as in Definition
7.10
).
In other words, the support of
\mathbf{m}
contains some antichain
\{(b_{1},c_{1}),\ldots,(b_{r},c_{r})\}
, written in order from northwest to southeast, such that
b_{r}
is less than the
t
th greatest entry of
T
.
Therefore, if we set
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{s},d_{1},\ldots,d_{t}\}
equal to
T
(so that
d_{1}
is the
t
th greatest entry of
T
), then
\mathbf{m}
is divisible by the monomial (
7.26
) of a split, and therefore is not a standard monomial.

On the other hand, suppose that the support of
\mathbf{m}
is contained in some jellyfish
\mathbf{F}\rightrightarrows T
.
Then there is no such
t
violating the width condition in (
7.29
), and so it is impossible to construct a split whose monomial divides
\mathbf{m}
.
Thus
\mathbf{m}
is a standard monomial.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.17
.

Starting from Lemma
7.13
, the proof is identical to those of Theorems
3.7
and
3.13
above, except for the shelling of
\Delta_{E}
.
In order to obtain a lexicographical shelling, we now view a lattice path as starting on the eastern edge of
\mathcal{P}
and ending on the diagonal.
Each step west is labeled “0,” and each step south is labeled “1.”
Since the endpoints are allowed to vary along the diagonal, the restriction of each facet
\mathbf{F}
is given not only by the set of descents in the label sequences, but also by any terminal 1’s in the label sequences of the paths in
\mathbf{F}
.
(This is because a terminal 1 is a descent in the symmetric lattice path obtained by reflecting about the diagonal.)
Hence the restriction of
\mathbf{F}
is precisely the set
{\rm cor}(\mathbf{F})
in (
3.13
).
The rest of the proof is identical to that of Theorem
3.13
.
∎

7.5.
Proof of Proposition
4.4

Recall that Proposition
4.4
interprets the weight of a monomial
\psi
(under the action of
\mathfrak{g}
) in terms of the degree sequence of the arc diagram of
\psi
.

Proof of Proposition
4.4
.

Let
H=\Sp_{2k}
.
Let
\psi=\mathbf{m}\cdot\varphi_{T}
, where
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}[f_{ij}]
is a monomial.
By the formula for
{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{m})

given in Section
A.2
,
each occurrence of
f_{ij}
in
\mathbf{m}
contributes
-\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}
to
{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\psi)
.
On one hand, then, each arc
\{i,j\}
in the arc diagram of
\psi
contributes 1 to
\deg i
and
\deg j
; on the other hand, since each arc
\{i,j\}
represents one occurrence of
f_{ij}
in
\mathbf{m}
, each contribution to
\deg i
actually contributes
-\epsilon_{i}
to
{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\psi)
.
Therefore the arcs together contribute
-{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{m})
to the degree sequence of the arc diagram.
It remains to show that the hyperedges together contribute
-{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\varphi_{T})
to the degree sequence.
On one hand, by definition, each dot within a hyperedge contributes 1 to the degree of the vertex directly above it.
On the other hand, recall from (
7.3
) that each entry
i
in
T
contributes
-\epsilon_{i}
to
{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\det_{T}\!X)
.
Since the isomorphism
\Psi
in Lemma
7.1
intertwines the
\mathfrak{g}
-action, it follows that each entry
i
in
T
contributes
-\epsilon_{i}
to
{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\varphi_{T})
.
But each entry
i
in
T
corresponds to one dot beneath vertex
i
in the arc diagram, and so indeed the hyperedges together contribute precisely
-{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\varphi_{T})
to the degree sequence of the arc diagram.
The proofs for
\GL_{k}
and
\operatorname{O}_{k}
are identical.
∎

Declarations of interest:
None.

Appendix A
Explicit maps in Howe duality settings

In the synopses below, we fix coordinate functions
x_{ij}
and
y_{ij}
on
W
.
We write
{\rm wt}_{H}
to denote the weight under the action of the maximal torus of the group
H
.
Likewise, we write
{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}
to denote the weight under the action of the Cartan subalgebra (consisting of diagonal matrices) of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
In order to express the weight of a monomial
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}[W]
, we write
\deg_{x_{i\bullet}}(\mathbf{m})
to denote the degree of
\mathbf{m}
in the variables
x_{i,1},x_{i,2},\ldots
, and we write
\deg_{x_{\bullet j}}(\mathbf{m})
to denote the degree of
\mathbf{m}
in the variables
x_{1,j},x_{2,j},\ldots
.

A.1.
Howe duality for
(H,\mathfrak{g})=(\GL_{k},\mathfrak{gl}_{p+q})

Upon fixing a basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}\}
for
V
and its dual basis
\{e^{*}_{1},\ldots,e^{*}_{k}\}
for
V^{*}
, we can view elements of
W=V^{*p}\oplus V^{q}
as ordered pairs
(Y,X)\in\M_{p,k}\oplus\M_{k,q}
.
Explicitly, the coordinate functions on
W
are given by

\displaystyle y_{ij}:(v^{*}_{1},\ldots,v^{*}_{p},v_{1},\ldots,v_{q})

\displaystyle\longmapsto v^{*}_{i}(e_{j}),\qquad(i,j)\in[p]\times[k],

\displaystyle x_{ij}:(v^{*}_{1},\ldots,v^{*}_{p},v_{1},\ldots,v_{q})

\displaystyle\longmapsto e^{*}_{i}(v_{j}),\qquad(i,j)\in[k]\times[q].

The action of
K\times H=\GL_{p}\times\GL_{q}\times\GL_{k}
on
\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{M}_{k,q}\oplus\operatorname{M}_{q,k}]
is given by

(g_{1},g_{2},h).f(Y,X)=\det(g_{1})^{-k}f(g_{1}^{-1}Yh,\>h^{-1}Xg_{2}).

In particular, the action of an element
t={\rm diag}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
in the maximal torus of
H=\GL_{k}
is given by

t.y_{ij}=t_{j}y_{ij},\qquad t.x_{ij}=t_{i}^{-1}x_{ij}.

With respect to this action, the weight of a monomial
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}[\M_{p,k}\oplus\M_{k,q}]
is given by the
k
-tuple

{\rm wt}_{H}(\mathbf{m})=\Big(\deg_{y_{\bullet 1}}(\mathbf{m})-\deg_{x_{1\bullet}}(\mathbf{m}),\ldots,\deg_{y_{\bullet k}}(\mathbf{m})-\deg_{x_{k\bullet}}(\mathbf{m})\Big).

The Lie algebra homomorphism
\omega:\mathfrak{gl}_{p+q}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}(\M_{p,k}\oplus\M_{k,q})^{\GL_{k}}
is given by

\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}\longmapsto&\ \sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}a_{ij}\left(-\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}y_{j\ell}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i\ell}}-k\delta_{ij}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}d_{ij}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}x_{\ell i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\ell j}}\right)\\
&\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{q}b_{ij}\bigg(-\underbrace{\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y_{i\ell}\partial x_{\ell j}}}_{\Delta_{ij}}\bigg)+\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{p}c_{ij}\bigg(\underbrace{\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}y_{j\ell}x_{\ell i}}_{f_{ji}}\bigg).\end{split}

With respect to this action, the weight of a monomial
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}[\M_{p,k}\oplus\M_{k,q}]
is given by the
(p+q)
-tuple

{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{m})=\Big(-\deg_{y_{1\bullet}}(\mathbf{m})-k,\ldots,-\deg_{y_{p\bullet}}(\mathbf{m})-k\>\Big|\>\deg_{x_{\bullet 1}}(\mathbf{m}),\ldots,\deg_{x_{\bullet q}}(\mathbf{m})\Big)

A.2.
Howe duality for
(H,\mathfrak{g})=(\Sp_{2k},\mathfrak{so}_{2n})

As above, one can view elements of
W=V^{n}
as matrices
X\in\M_{2k,n}
, with coordinate functions given by

\displaystyle x_{ij}:(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})

\displaystyle\longmapsto e^{*}_{i}(v_{j}),\qquad(i,j)\in[2k]\times[n].

The action of
K\times H=\GL_{n}\times\Sp_{2k}
on
\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{M}_{2k,n}]
is given by

(g,h).f(X)=\det(g)^{-k}f(h^{-1}X(g^{-1})^{t}).

In particular, the action of an element
t={\rm diag}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k},t_{1}^{-1},\ldots,t_{k}^{-1})
of the maximal torus of
H=\Sp_{2k}
is given by

t.x_{ij}=\begin{cases}t_{i}^{-1}x_{ij},&1\leq i\leq k,\\
t_{i}x_{ij},&k<i\leq 2k.\end{cases}

With respect to this action, the weight of a monomial
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}[\M_{2k,n}]
is given by the
k
-tuple

{\rm wt}_{H}(\mathbf{m})=\Big(\deg_{x_{k+1,\bullet}}(\mathbf{m})-\deg_{x_{1,\bullet}}(\mathbf{m}),\deg_{x_{k+2,\bullet}}(\mathbf{m})-\deg_{x_{2,\bullet}}(\mathbf{m}),\ldots,\deg_{x_{2k,\bullet}}(\mathbf{m})-\deg_{x_{k,\bullet}}(\mathbf{m})\Big).

The Lie algebra homomorphism
\omega:\mathfrak{so}_{2n}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}(\M_{2k,n})^{\Sp_{2k}}
is given by

\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&-A^{t}\end{pmatrix}\longmapsto&\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{ij}\bigg(-\sum_{\ell=1}^{2k}x_{\ell j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\ell i}}-k\delta_{ij}\bigg)\\
&\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ij}\bigg(-\frac{1}{4}\underbrace{\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{\ell i}\partial x_{\ell+k,j}}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{\ell+k,i}\partial x_{\ell j}}\Big)}_{\Delta_{ij}}\bigg)\\[-10.0pt]
&\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{ij}\bigg(\underbrace{\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\Big(x_{\ell j}x_{\ell+k,i}-x_{\ell+k,j}x_{\ell i}\Big)}_{f_{ji}}\bigg).\end{split}

With respect to this action, the weight of a monomial
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}[\M_{2k},n]
is given by the
n
-tuple

{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{m})=\Big(-\deg_{x_{\bullet 1}}(\mathbf{m})-k,\ldots,-\deg_{x_{\bullet n}}(\mathbf{m})-k\Big).

A.3.
Howe duality for
(H,\mathfrak{g})=(\operatorname{O}_{k},\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})

As above, one can view elements of
W=V^{n}
as matrices
X\in\M_{k,n}
, with coordinate functions given by

\displaystyle x_{ij}:(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})

\displaystyle\longmapsto e^{*}_{i}(v_{j}),\qquad(i,j)\in[k]\times[n].

The action of
K\times H=\widetilde{\GL}_{n}\times\operatorname{O}_{k}
on
\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{M}_{k,n}]
is given by

((g,s),h).f(X)=s^{-k}f(h^{-1}X(g^{-1})^{t}),

where
\widetilde{\GL}_{n}:=\{(g,s)\in\GL_{n}\times\,\mathbb{C}^{\times}:\det(g)=s^{2}\}

and hence
s^{-k}
can be interpreted as “
\,\det(g)^{-k/2}
.”
The Lie algebra homomorphism
\omega:\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}(\operatorname{M}_{k,n})^{\operatorname{O}_{k}}
is given by

\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&-A^{t}\end{pmatrix}\longmapsto&\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{ij}\bigg(-\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}x_{\ell j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\ell i}}-\frac{k}{2}\delta_{ij}\bigg)\\
&\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ij}\bigg(-\frac{1}{4}\underbrace{\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{\ell i}\partial x_{\ell j}}}_{\Delta_{ij}}\bigg)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{ij}\bigg(\underbrace{\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}x_{\ell j}x_{\ell i}}_{f_{ji}}\bigg).\end{split}

With respect to this action, the weight of a monomial
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}[\M_{k,n}]
is given by the
n
-tuple

{\rm wt}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{m})=\Big(-\deg_{x_{\bullet 1}}(\mathbf{m})-\tfrac{k}{2},\ldots,-\deg_{x_{\bullet n}}(\mathbf{m})-\tfrac{k}{2}\Big).

A.4.
Maps between coordinate functions

In each of the three cases above, identify
\mathfrak{p}^{-}
with
(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{*}
via the
K
-equivariant linear isomorphism

\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
C&0\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\operatorname{trace}(CB)\right].

Then define the matrix coordinate functions
z_{ij}\in(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{*}
by

z_{ij}:\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\mapsto b_{ij}.

Via the inverse isomorphism
(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{*}\rightarrow\mathfrak{p}^{-}
,
we have

z_{ij}\mapsto\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
E_{ji}&0\end{pmatrix}&\mbox{if $\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{p+q}$ and $1\leq i\leq p$, $1\leq j\leq q$;}\\[20.0pt]
\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\frac{1}{2}(E_{ji}-E_{ij})&0\end{pmatrix}&\mbox{if $\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}_{2n}$ and $1\leq i<j\leq n$;}\\[20.0pt]
\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\frac{1}{2}(E_{ji}+E_{ij})&0\end{pmatrix}&\mbox{if $\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}$ and $1\leq i\leq j\leq n$.}\end{cases}

It follows that in all three Howe duality settings above, we have

z_{ij}\mapsto f_{ij}

via
\mathbb{C}[z_{ij}]=\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{p}^{+}]\cong\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{p}^{-})\subset\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(W)^{H}
.
